They take the long way around the hallway just so they don't have to walk past my door. It’s ridiculous, they’re being overly sensitive, but... yeah, I guess I've been pretty irritable.\n\nDoctor: Have you been needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh, sleep is... honestly, it's just such an inefficient interruption right now. Most nights I'm only getting maybe two or three hours. I'll just crash on the couch with my laptop still open, usually right in the middle of analyzing data. And the crazy thing is, I wake up completely sharp. Just boom, ready to go. No grogginess, no need for coffee. \n\nSpeaking of coffee, they switched the beans in the faculty lounge again. It used to be this really robust dark roast, and now it's some weak, acidic nonsense that tastes like battery acid. I sent an email to the department head about it, but of course, he ignored it. Just like he ignored my proposal to reorganize the shared equipment schedule. It's like nobody cares about optimizing our resources! I mean, if we just shifted the NMR machine usage blocks by two hours, we could increase throughput by at least twenty percent. I mapped it all out on a spreadsheet the other night when I was awake at 3 AM. It’s so obvious, but getting anyone to actually look at it is impossible. So yeah, I don't really need much sleep lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t?\n\nPatient: Hearing or seeing things? No, no, nothing like that. My mind is perfectly clear, clearer than it's been in years, actually. It's just... well, it's the noise. Not like hallucinations, just the actual noise in the building. The HVAC system in the new science wing has this persistent high-frequency whine that nobody else seems to notice, or maybe they just don't care. It's exactly at 14,000 hertz. I actually brought in an acoustic meter to measure it because I thought I was going crazy, but no, it's real. \n\nAnd the administration won't do anything about it. They spent forty million dollars on that building, and they went with the lowest bidder for the ventilation contractors. Typical. It's just like how they handled the parking situation last semester. They completely repainted the lots, changed all the designated zones, and suddenly my permit, which I've had for twelve years, is only valid in the lot that's a half-mile walk from my lab. I tried explaining to parking services that I need to be able to check on my samples at odd hours, but they just handed me a pamphlet. A pamphlet! So no, I'm not seeing things, I'm just seeing how incredibly incompetent everyone around me is right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any beliefs lately that other people say are not true or seem unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Unrealistic? I mean, they say it's unrealistic, but that's just because they don't have the vision for it. I genuinely believed—and I still think I could do it if they'd just let me—that I could rescue the entire multi-site project completely by myself. I mean, the current spectroscopy approach they're using is fundamentally flawed, and I knew I could redesign the whole thing. Plus, I could mentor every single grad student better than their current advisors are doing, and still easily beat the grant deadline. \n\nBut of course, people just keep getting in my way. Which is exactly what happened with the departmental retreat last year. They put Susan in charge of the itinerary, and Susan... well, Susan thinks a \"productive retreat\" means trust falls and making collages out of old science magazines. I told her we needed to be running mock grant review panels, and she looked at me like I had two heads. I ended up skipping the whole afternoon session just to rewrite the lab safety protocols because someone had to do something useful. And don't even get me started on the catering for that retreat. They ordered from that place on 5th Street, the one that always undercooks the chicken. I got food poisoning and was out for three days, which is probably the only reason Susan managed to get her poster submitted on time, because I wasn't there to point out the glaring statistical errors in her methodology. But anyway, yeah, they tell me my plans for the project are \"unrealistic.\" It's just small-mindedness, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you believed lately that you have special abilities or powers that other people do not have?\n\nPatient: Special abilities? Like... magic powers or something? No, I don't think I'm a superhero. I mean, I'm just incredibly capable, much more than anyone else in my department, but that's just because I actually apply myself and understand the raw data. It's not a superpower, it's just basic competence, which seems like a superpower to them because the bar is so incredibly low. \n\nIt's exactly like what happened when they tried to upgrade the server infrastructure for the computational chemistry group. They hired this outside IT consultant who charged, I don't even want to know, probably three hundred dollars an hour. And he comes in, looks at our server racks, and suggests we migrate everything to this cloud service that has a known latency issue with the specific modeling software we use. I told him this. I literally pulled up the documentation on my phone and showed him the exact paragraph where it says it's not compatible. And he just smiled this condescending little smile and said, \"We'll optimize it.\" Optimize it! You can't optimize a fundamental incompatibility! I ended up having to write a custom script to bypass the bottleneck he created, which took me an entire weekend. A weekend I was supposed to spend at my sister's house for her anniversary barbecue. Which I missed, and she's still mad about it, even though I told her I was literally saving the department from a massive data bottleneck. She just doesn't understand the pressure I'm under. So, no, no special powers. Just... having to do everyone else's job for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special mission or destiny that other people do not share?\n\nPatient: A special mission? No, I wouldn't call it a destiny or a mission or anything grand like that. I just have a job to do, and I'm the only one who actually sees what needs to be done to keep this department from completely collapsing. It's not a destiny, it's just... taking responsibility. Which is a completely foreign concept to the administration. \n\nJust look at the budget allocation for next fiscal year. They decided to cut the travel funding for the post-docs by thirty percent. Thirty percent! How are they supposed to present their findings? How are they supposed to network? I went straight to the Dean's office—didn't even bother with an appointment because his secretary, Linda, is impossible to deal with. She always acts like she's guarding the crown jewels instead of a guy who spends half his day playing golf. Anyway, I marched right in there and laid out exactly how this cut was going to destroy our research output over the next five years. I even drew a graph on his whiteboard. He just sat there blinking at me like I was speaking a different language. And then he had the nerve to ask me if I was feeling stressed. Stressed? I'm trying to save the future of the department and he's asking about my stress levels. It's unbelievable. So no, no special destiny, just a desperate attempt to keep the ship from sinking while the captain is out on the back nine.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have abilities far beyond what is realistically possible for other people?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if I'd say \"far beyond what is realistically possible,\" because to me, it's completely realistic. I already told you about the multi-site project, right? I genuinely believed—and I still do—that I could rescue the whole thing completely by myself. Redesign the entire spectroscopy approach from the ground up, mentor every single student in the lab better than their actual advisors, and still easily beat the grant deadline. It's totally possible if people would just stop getting in my way. \n\nWhich is exactly the problem with the new safety committee they just formed. They put Marcus on it, and Marcus is terrified of his own shadow. He wants to require full face shields and double-gloving for handling dilute saline solutions. Saline! It's basically salt water! I tried to explain to him that he's creating a culture of compliance fatigue, where people are just going to start ignoring all the rules because the rules are so absurd. But he wouldn't listen. He just kept quoting this OSHA manual from 1998. I actually had to go over his head and talk to the facilities manager, who by the way, has his own issues. He's been promising to fix the HVAC in the south wing for six months, and my office is freezing. I have to wear a fleece jacket indoors in July. It's ridiculous. But anyway, yeah, my abilities are completely realistic, it's just that everyone else's abilities are... lacking.\n\nDoctor: Have these changes caused serious problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Serious problems? I mean, they're making it a problem. My department actually had to step in recently and take over some of the decisions I used to handle. They claimed I was missing priorities and confusing the scheduling, which is ridiculous because my schedule makes perfect sense if you actually look at the big picture. They said my supervision was strained and putting the project and lab safety expectations at risk. Lab safety! As if I don't know how to run a lab. \n\nIt's just like the time I tried to organize the neighborhood block party last summer. I had this whole vision for it, right? I was going to get a live band, a petting zoo for the kids, and coordinate a massive potluck where everyone had to bring a dish from a different country. I even made this huge spreadsheet to track everything. But then Mrs. Gable from down the street—she's the head of the HOA and honestly, she has way too much time on her hands—she started complaining that we didn't have the proper permits for the petting zoo. Permits! For a few goats and a pony! I told her I'd handle it, but she just went ahead and canceled the whole thing behind my back. She said I was being \"unrealistic\" and \"overwhelming\" everyone. Overwhelming? I was doing all the work! Just like at the lab. People just can't handle it when someone actually has vision and tries to elevate things beyond the bare minimum. They just want to drag you down to their level of mediocrity.\n\nDoctor: Do other people find your beliefs about what you can accomplish to be impossible or clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: Impossible? I mean, I don't know. I haven't really noticed anyone saying that to me. I'm not sure what they actually think behind closed doors, to be honest. People don't really say things to your face anymore, they just... they just smile and nod. \n\nWhich is exactly what happened with the contractor who was supposed to renovate my kitchen last month. He stood right there in my house, looked at the blueprints I drew up—which were perfectly scaled, by the way, down to the millimeter—and said, \"Sure, no problem.\" Next thing I know, he's leaving me a voicemail saying the custom cabinets are delayed because of some supply chain issue in Italy. Italy! I called him back and asked why we're sourcing wood from Italy when there's a perfectly good commercial lumber yard two towns over. But he started giving me this whole lecture about European craftsmanship and the moisture content of the wood. Moisture content! I'm a physical scientist, I think I understand how humidity affects porous materials better than a guy who drives a pickup truck with a cracked windshield. \n\nAnd speaking of windshields, the parking situation on campus has gotten completely out of hand. They repainted the lines in the North lot over the weekend and somehow managed to eliminate fifteen spaces. Fifteen! Where are the adjuncts supposed to park? I had to circle for twenty minutes on Tuesday. It's just... it's a complete mess.\n\nDoctor: Have you believed you could do something that would be impossible for any ordinary person?\n\nPatient: Impossible for an ordinary person? I mean, what even is an ordinary person? I guess I don't really think about it like that. I don't think I've ever believed I could do something literally impossible, like... like flying or something. I just do what I know I can do. \n\nWhich is why I still don't understand why they wouldn't let me install the new centrifuge myself. I mean, it's just a matter of calibrating the rotor balance and securing the mounting bolts. I've read the entire technical manual, twice. But they insisted on calling the manufacturer's technician, who didn't even show up until three days later. Three days! And when he finally got there, he didn't even use a torque wrench on the base plate. I watched him just hand-tighten it. I actually had to go back in after he left and do it properly myself because I'm not going to have a piece of equipment spinning at 15,000 RPMs secured by some guy who looked like he was hungover. \n\nAnd honestly, the coffee in the breakroom isn't helping anyone's productivity either. They switched to this new generic brand last month to save money, and it tastes like burnt cardboard. I actually brought in my own French press and a bag of Ethiopian Yirgacheffe, but then someone—I'm pretty sure it was Dave from the next lab over—broke the glass beaker and didn't even leave a note. Just threw it in the trash. The lack of basic respect for shared spaces is just... it's astounding. But no, I don't think I've tried to do anything impossible. Just things that need to be done right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that people are talking about you, watching you, or trying to interfere with you in ways others would say aren't true?\n\nPatient: Watching me? I mean... no, I don't think so. I don't think there's some big conspiracy or anything like that. People are way too self-absorbed to spend their time secretly watching me or plotting against me. I mean, they interfere, sure, but just out of sheer incompetence, not because it's some organized campaign. \n\nLike Brenda down in the department office. She's supposed to sort the incoming lab packages, right? But last week, a shipment of specialized thermal paste I needed for the heat sinks just sat on her desk for four days. Four days! And when I finally went down there to find it myself, she didn't even apologize. She just started rambling about her daughter's wedding. Which she has been talking about non-stop since January, by the way. I don't even know why they're insisting on an outdoor reception in late October. Do you know how unstable the barometric pressure is around here that time of year? It's almost guaranteed to rain. And the tent rental company she hired—I looked them up—they have terrible reviews for not anchoring their tie-downs properly in saturated soil. If they get a wind gust over thirty miles an hour, that whole canopy is going to become a giant sail. I actually tried to explain the physics of it to her, just to be helpful, you know? Just drawing out a basic vector diagram on a sticky note. But she just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. People just do not appreciate basic meteorology.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that ordinary events were sending you special messages meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Special messages? No, I don't think so. I mean, events are just events, right? They happen because of cause and effect, basic physics. There's no magical meaning behind a traffic light turning green or... or a bird landing on a windowsill. It's just probability and environmental factors. \n\nSpeaking of environmental factors, the HVAC system in my lab has been an absolute nightmare lately. The temperature fluctuates by almost three degrees throughout the day, which completely throws off my calibration curves. I've put in six work orders since Tuesday. Six! And the maintenance guy—Gary, I think his name is—finally shows up yesterday and tells me the system is \"working within normal parameters.\" Normal parameters! I had to pull up the sensor logs on my laptop and show him the exact timestamps of the thermal spikes. I even color-coded the graph so it would be impossible to miss. But he just kept wiping his forehead with this greasy rag and talking about how the compressor on the roof is twenty years old. \n\nWell, if it's twenty years old, replace it! It's not like the university doesn't have the endowment for it. Did you see the new bronze statue they just put up in front of the library? It must have cost at least eighty thousand dollars. And it's not even structurally sound! The center of gravity is completely off. I give it two harsh winters before the frost heave tilts the pedestal and the whole thing comes crashing down. I actually wrote an email to the dean of facilities about the tensile strength of the mounting brackets they used, but of course, no one ever replies to me. So no, I don't get any special messages from the universe. I just wish people would read the messages I actually send them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Impossible? I mean, maybe someone said that, but they just don't see the big picture. They told me I was taking on too much, but I genuinely believed I could rescue the entire multi-site project by myself. It was failing, obviously failing, because the other PIs were dragging their feet. I knew I could redesign the whole spectroscopy approach, mentor every single grad student better than their actual advisors were doing, and still beat the grant deadline. It was totally doable if people would just stop getting in my way. 

Which, speaking of getting in the way, you wouldn't believe the traffic on I-95 this morning. It's like people forget how to use their turn signals the second they get on the highway. There was this one guy in a silver sedan who merged across three lanes without even looking. Just drifted right over. It's basic spatial awareness, really. If you're traveling at sixty-five miles per hour, your stopping distance is roughly three hundred feet. You can't just cut into someone's following distance and expect physics to magically suspend itself for you. 

And then, of course, the toll booth lanes were completely backed up because half the automated scanners were down. I don't understand why the state doesn't upgrade the RFID readers. The technology has been around for decades. I actually read a white paper last month about a new high-frequency transponder system they're using in Europe that has a 99.9% read accuracy even at high speeds. I mean, I could probably draft a proposal for the Department of Transportation in a weekend if I really sat down and did it. It's just a matter of optimizing the signal processing algorithms. But anyway, yeah, I could have saved that project if they had just let me handle it.

Oh, sleep is... honestly, it's just such an inefficient interruption right now. Most nights I'm only getting maybe two or three hours. It used to be this really robust dark roast, and now it's some weak, acidic nonsense that tastes like battery acid. I sent an email to the department head about it, but of course, he ignored it. Just like he ignored my proposal to reorganize the shared equipment schedule. It's like nobody cares about optimizing our resources! I mean, if we just shifted the NMR machine usage blocks by two hours, we could increase throughput by at least twenty percent. I mapped it all out on a spreadsheet the other night when I was awake at 3 AM. It’s so obvious, but getting anyone to actually look at it is impossible. So yeah, I don't really need much sleep lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t?\n\nPatient: Hearing or seeing things? No, no, nothing like that. My mind is perfectly clear, clearer than it's been in years, actually. It's just... well, it's the noise. Not like hallucinations, just the actual noise in the building. The HVAC system in the new science wing has this persistent high-frequency whine that nobody else seems to notice, or maybe they just don't care. It's exactly at 14,000 hertz. I actually brought in an acoustic meter to measure it because I thought I was going crazy, but no, it's real. \n\nAnd the administration won't do anything about it. They spent forty million dollars on that building, and they went with the lowest bidder for the ventilation contractors. Typical. It's just like how they handled the parking situation last semester. They completely repainted the lots, changed all the designated zones, and suddenly my permit, which I've had for twelve years, is only valid in the lot that's a half-mile walk from my lab. I tried explaining to parking services that I need to be able to check on my samples at odd hours, but they just handed me a pamphlet. A pamphlet! So no, I'm not seeing things, I'm just seeing how incredibly incompetent everyone around me is right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any beliefs lately that other people say are not true or seem unrealistic?\n\nPatient: Unrealistic? I mean, they say it's unrealistic, but that's just because they don't have the vision for it. I genuinely believed—and I still think I could do it if they'd just let me—that I could rescue the entire multi-site project completely by myself. I mean, the current spectroscopy approach they're using is fundamentally flawed, and I knew I could redesign the whole thing. Plus, I could mentor every single grad student better than their current advisors are doing, and still easily beat the grant deadline. \n\nBut of course, people just keep getting in my way. Which is exactly what happened with the departmental retreat last year. They put Susan in charge of the itinerary, and Susan... well, Susan thinks a \"productive retreat\" means trust falls and making collages out of old science magazines. I told her we needed to be running mock grant review panels, and she looked at me like I had two heads. I ended up skipping the whole afternoon session just to rewrite the lab safety protocols because someone had to do something useful. And don't even get me started on the catering for that retreat. They ordered from that place on 5th Street, the one that always undercooks the chicken. I got food poisoning and was out for three days, which is probably the only reason Susan managed to get her poster submitted on time, because I wasn't there to point out the glaring statistical errors in her methodology. But anyway, yeah, they tell me my plans for the project are \"unrealistic.\" It's just small-mindedness, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you believed lately that you have special abilities or powers that other people do not have?\n\nPatient: Special abilities? Like... magic powers or something? No, I don't think I'm a superhero. I mean, I'm just incredibly capable, much more than anyone else in my department, but that's just because I actually apply myself and understand the raw data. It's not a superpower, it's just basic competence, which seems like a superpower to them because the bar is so incredibly low. \n\nIt's exactly like what happened when they tried to upgrade the server infrastructure for the computational chemistry group. They hired this outside IT consultant who charged, I don't even want to know, probably three hundred dollars an hour. And he comes in, looks at our server racks, and suggests we migrate everything to this cloud service that has a known latency issue with the specific modeling software we use. I told him this. I literally pulled up the documentation on my phone and showed him the exact paragraph where it says it's not compatible. And he just smiled this condescending little smile and said, \"We'll optimize it.\" Optimize it! You can't optimize a fundamental incompatibility! I ended up having to write a custom script to bypass the bottleneck he created, which took me an entire weekend. A weekend I was supposed to spend at my sister's house for her anniversary barbecue. Which I missed, and she's still mad about it, even though I told her I was literally saving the department from a massive data bottleneck. She just doesn't understand the pressure I'm under. So, no, no special powers. Just... having to do everyone else's job for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have a special mission or destiny that other people do not share?\n\nPatient: A special mission? No, I wouldn't call it a destiny or a mission or anything grand like that. I just have a job to do, and I'm the only one who actually sees what needs to be done to keep this department from completely collapsing. It's not a destiny, it's just... taking responsibility. Which is a completely foreign concept to the administration. \n\nJust look at the budget allocation for next fiscal year. They decided to cut the travel funding for the post-docs by thirty percent. Thirty percent! How are they supposed to present their findings? How are they supposed to network? I went straight to the Dean's office—didn't even bother with an appointment because his secretary, Linda, is impossible to deal with. She always acts like she's guarding the crown jewels instead of a guy who spends half his day playing golf. Anyway, I marched right in there and laid out exactly how this cut was going to destroy our research output over the next five years. I even drew a graph on his whiteboard. He just sat there blinking at me like I was speaking a different language. And then he had the nerve to ask me if I was feeling stressed. Stressed? I'm trying to save the future of the department and he's asking about my stress levels. It's unbelievable. So no, no special destiny, just a desperate attempt to keep the ship from sinking while the captain is out on the back nine.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe you have abilities far beyond what is realistically possible for other people?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't know if I'd say \"far beyond what is realistically possible,\" because to me, it's completely realistic. I already told you about the multi-site project, right? I genuinely believed—and I still do—that I could rescue the whole thing completely by myself. Redesign the entire spectroscopy approach from the ground up, mentor every single student in the lab better than their actual advisors, and still easily beat the grant deadline. It's totally possible if people would just stop getting in my way. \n\nWhich is exactly the problem with the new safety committee they just formed. They put Marcus on it, and Marcus is terrified of his own shadow. He wants to require full face shields and double-gloving for handling dilute saline solutions. Saline! It's basically salt water! I tried to explain to him that he's creating a culture of compliance fatigue, where people are just going to start ignoring all the rules because the rules are so absurd. But he wouldn't listen. He just kept quoting this OSHA manual from 1998. I actually had to go over his head and talk to the facilities manager, who by the way, has his own issues. He's been promising to fix the HVAC in the south wing for six months, and my office is freezing. I have to wear a fleece jacket indoors in July. It's ridiculous. But anyway, yeah, my abilities are completely realistic, it's just that everyone else's abilities are... lacking.\n\nDoctor: Have these changes caused serious problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Serious problems? I mean, they're making it a problem. My department actually had to step in recently and take over some of the decisions I used to handle. They claimed I was missing priorities and confusing the scheduling, which is ridiculous because my schedule makes perfect sense if you actually look at the big picture. They said my supervision was strained and putting the project and lab safety expectations at risk. Lab safety! As if I don't know how to run a lab. \n\nIt's just like the time I tried to organize the neighborhood block party last summer. I had this whole vision for it, right? I was going to get a live band, a petting zoo for the kids, and coordinate a massive potluck where everyone had to bring a dish from a different country. I even made this huge spreadsheet to track everything. But then Mrs. Gable from down the street—she's the head of the HOA and honestly, she has way too much time on her hands—she started complaining that we didn't have the proper permits for the petting zoo. Permits! For a few goats and a pony! I told her I'd handle it, but she just went ahead and canceled the whole thing behind my back. She said I was being \"unrealistic\" and \"overwhelming\" everyone. Overwhelming? I was doing all the work! Just like at the lab. People just can't handle it when someone actually has vision and tries to elevate things beyond the bare minimum. They just want to drag you down to their level of mediocrity.\n\nDoctor: Do other people find your beliefs about what you can accomplish to be impossible or clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: Impossible? I mean, I don't know. I haven't really noticed anyone saying that to me. I'm not sure what they actually think behind closed doors, to be honest. People don't really say things to your face anymore, they just... they just smile and nod. \n\nWhich is exactly what happened with the contractor who was supposed to renovate my kitchen last month. He stood right there in my house, looked at the blueprints I drew up—which were perfectly scaled, by the way, down to the millimeter—and said, \"Sure, no problem.\" Next thing I know, he's leaving me a voicemail saying the custom cabinets are delayed because of some supply chain issue in Italy. Italy! I called him back and asked why we're sourcing wood from Italy when there's a perfectly good commercial lumber yard two towns over. But he started giving me this whole lecture about European craftsmanship and the moisture content of the wood. Moisture content! I'm a physical scientist, I think I understand how humidity affects porous materials better than a guy who drives a pickup truck with a cracked windshield. \n\nAnd speaking of windshields, the parking situation on campus has gotten completely out of hand. They repainted the lines in the North lot over the weekend and somehow managed to eliminate fifteen spaces. Fifteen! Where are the adjuncts supposed to park? I had to circle for twenty minutes on Tuesday. It's just... it's a complete mess.\n\nDoctor: Have you believed you could do something that would be impossible for any ordinary person?\n\nPatient: Impossible for an ordinary person? I mean, what even is an ordinary person? I guess I don't really think about it like that. I don't think I've ever believed I could do something literally impossible, like... like flying or something. I just do what I know I can do. \n\nWhich is why I still don't understand why they wouldn't let me install the new centrifuge myself. I mean, it's just a matter of calibrating the rotor balance and securing the mounting bolts. I've read the entire technical manual, twice. But they insisted on calling the manufacturer's technician, who didn't even show up until three days later. Three days! And when he finally got there, he didn't even use a torque wrench on the base plate. I watched him just hand-tighten it. I actually had to go back in after he left and do it properly myself because I'm not going to have a piece of equipment spinning at 15,000 RPMs secured by some guy who looked like he was hungover. \n\nAnd honestly, the coffee in the breakroom isn't helping anyone's productivity either. They switched to this new generic brand last month to save money, and it tastes like burnt cardboard. I actually brought in my own French press and a bag of Ethiopian Yirgacheffe, but then someone—I'm pretty sure it was Dave from the next lab over—broke the glass beaker and didn't even leave a note. Just threw it in the trash. The lack of basic respect for shared spaces is just... it's astounding. But no, I don't think I've tried to do anything impossible. Just things that need to be done right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that people are talking about you, watching you, or trying to interfere with you in ways others would say aren't true?\n\nPatient: Watching me? I mean... no, I don't think so. I don't think there's some big conspiracy or anything like that. People are way too self-absorbed to spend their time secretly watching me or plotting against me. I mean, they interfere, sure, but just out of sheer incompetence, not because it's some organized campaign. \n\nLike Brenda down in the department office. She's supposed to sort the incoming lab packages, right? But last week, a shipment of specialized thermal paste I needed for the heat sinks just sat on her desk for four days. Four days! And when I finally went down there to find it myself, she didn't even apologize. She just started rambling about her daughter's wedding. Which she has been talking about non-stop since January, by the way. I don't even know why they're insisting on an outdoor reception in late October. Do you know how unstable the barometric pressure is around here that time of year? It's almost guaranteed to rain. And the tent rental company she hired—I looked them up—they have terrible reviews for not anchoring their tie-downs properly in saturated soil. If they get a wind gust over thirty miles an hour, that whole canopy is going to become a giant sail. I actually tried to explain the physics of it to her, just to be helpful, you know? Just drawing out a basic vector diagram on a sticky note. But she just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. People just do not appreciate basic meteorology.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that ordinary events were sending you special messages meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Special messages? No, I don't think so. I mean, events are just events, right? They happen because of cause and effect, basic physics. There's no magical meaning behind a traffic light turning green or... or a bird landing on a windowsill. It's just probability and environmental factors. \n\nSpeaking of environmental factors, the HVAC system in my lab has been an absolute nightmare lately. The temperature fluctuates by almost three degrees throughout the day, which completely throws off my calibration curves. I've put in six work orders since Tuesday. Six! And the maintenance guy—Gary, I think his name is—finally shows up yesterday and tells me the system is \"working within normal parameters.\" Normal parameters! I had to pull up the sensor logs on my laptop and show him the exact timestamps of the thermal spikes. I even color-coded the graph so it would be impossible to miss. But he just kept wiping his forehead with this greasy rag and talking about how the compressor on the roof is twenty years old. \n\nWell, if it's twenty years old, replace it! It's not like the university doesn't have the endowment for it. Did you see the new bronze statue they just put up in front of the library? It must have cost at least eighty thousand dollars. And it's not even structurally sound! The center of gravity is completely off. I give it two harsh winters before the frost heave tilts the pedestal and the whole thing comes crashing down. I actually wrote an email to the dean of facilities about the tensile strength of the mounting brackets they used, but of course, no one ever replies to me. So no, I don't get any special messages from the universe. I just wish people would read the messages I actually send them.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that your beliefs about what you can accomplish are clearly impossible?\n\nPatient: Impossible? I mean, maybe someone said that, but they just don't see the big picture. They told me I was taking on too much, but I genuinely believed I could rescue the entire multi-site project by myself. It was failing, obviously failing, because the other PIs were dragging their feet. I knew I could redesign the whole spectroscopy approach, mentor every single grad student better than their actual advisors were doing, and still beat the grant deadline. It was totally doable if people would just stop getting in my way. \n\nWhich, speaking of getting in the way, you wouldn't believe the traffic on I-95 this morning. It's like people forget how to use their turn signals the second they get on the highway. There was this one guy in a silver sedan who merged across three lanes without even looking. Just drifted right over. It's basic spatial awareness, really. If you're traveling at sixty-five miles per hour, your stopping distance is roughly three hundred feet. You can't just cut into someone's following distance and expect physics to magically suspend itself for you. \n\nAnd then, of course, the toll booth lanes were completely backed up because half the automated scanners were down. I don't understand why the state doesn't upgrade the RFID readers. The technology has been around for decades. I actually read a white paper last month about a new high-frequency transponder system they're using in Europe that has a 99.9% read accuracy even at high speeds. I mean, I could probably draft a proposal for the Department of Transportation in a weekend if I really sat down and did it. It's just a matter of optimizing the signal processing algorithms. But anyway, yeah, I could have saved that project if they had just let me handle it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

False or impossible? No, I don't think so. I mean, my ideas are grounded in reality, in data. I don't just make things up. If I say I can do something, it's because I've run the numbers and I know it's feasible. People might not understand the math, but that doesn't make it false.

I actually stood in the aisle and calculated the price per ounce difference on my phone. It's a twenty-five percent increase in cost for twenty-five percent less product. 

And don't even get me started on the produce section. The avocados were rock hard, and they were charging two-fifty each. Who pays two-fifty for an avocado that won't even be ripe for a week? By the time it softens up, the inside is usually all stringy and brown anyway. It's a complete gamble. I actually read an article about how they're trying to genetically modify avocados to stay ripe longer without oxidizing, which is fascinating from a biochemical standpoint. As if I don't know how to run a lab. \n\nIt's just like the time I tried to organize the neighborhood block party last summer. I had this whole vision for it, right? I was going to get a live band, a petting zoo for the kids, and coordinate a massive potluck where everyone had to bring a dish from a different country. I even made this huge spreadsheet to track everything. But then Mrs. Gable from down the street—she's the head of the HOA and honestly, she has way too much time on her hands—she started complaining that we didn't have the proper permits for the petting zoo. Permits! For a few goats and a pony! I told her I'd handle it, but she just went ahead and canceled the whole thing behind my back. She said I was being \"unrealistic\" and \"overwhelming\" everyone. Overwhelming? I was doing all the work! Just like at the lab. People just can't handle it when someone actually has vision and tries to elevate things beyond the bare minimum. They just want to drag you down to their level of mediocrity.\n\nDoctor: Do other people find your beliefs about what you can accomplish to be impossible or clearly untrue?\n\nPatient: Impossible? I mean, I don't know. I haven't really noticed anyone saying that to me. I'm not sure what they actually think behind closed doors, to be honest. People don't really say things to your face anymore, they just... they just smile and nod. \n\nWhich is exactly what happened with the contractor who was supposed to renovate my kitchen last month. He stood right there in my house, looked at the blueprints I drew up—which were perfectly scaled, by the way, down to the millimeter—and said, \"Sure, no problem.\" Next thing I know, he's leaving me a voicemail saying the custom cabinets are delayed because of some supply chain issue in Italy. Italy! I called him back and asked why we're sourcing wood from Italy when there's a perfectly good commercial lumber yard two towns over. But he started giving me this whole lecture about European craftsmanship and the moisture content of the wood. Moisture content! I'm a physical scientist, I think I understand how humidity affects porous materials better than a guy who drives a pickup truck with a cracked windshield. \n\nAnd speaking of windshields, the parking situation on campus has gotten completely out of hand. They repainted the lines in the North lot over the weekend and somehow managed to eliminate fifteen spaces. Fifteen! Where are the adjuncts supposed to park? I had to circle for twenty minutes on Tuesday. It's just... it's a complete mess.\n\nDoctor: Have you believed you could do something that would be impossible for any ordinary person?\n\nPatient: Impossible for an ordinary person? I mean, what even is an ordinary person? I guess I don't really think about it like that. I don't think I've ever believed I could do something literally impossible, like... like flying or something. I just do what I know I can do. \n\nWhich is why I still don't understand why they wouldn't let me install the new centrifuge myself. I mean, it's just a matter of calibrating the rotor balance and securing the mounting bolts. I've read the entire technical manual, twice. But they insisted on calling the manufacturer's technician, who didn't even show up until three days later. Three days! And when he finally got there, he didn't even use a torque wrench on the base plate. I watched him just hand-tighten it. I actually had to go back in after he left and do it properly myself because I'm not going to have a piece of equipment spinning at 15,000 RPMs secured by some guy who looked like he was hungover. \n\nAnd honestly, the coffee in the breakroom isn't helping anyone's productivity either. They switched to this new generic brand last month to save money, and it tastes like burnt cardboard. I actually brought in my own French press and a bag of Ethiopian Yirgacheffe, but then someone—I'm pretty sure it was Dave from the next lab over—broke the glass beaker and didn't even leave a note. Just threw it in the trash. The lack of basic respect for shared spaces is just... it's astounding. But no, I don't think I've tried to do anything impossible. Just things that need to be done right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that people are talking about you, watching you, or trying to interfere with you in ways others would say aren't true?\n\nPatient: Watching me? I mean... no, I don't think so. I don't think there's some big conspiracy or anything like that. People are way too self-absorbed to spend their time secretly watching me or plotting against me. I mean, they interfere, sure, but just out of sheer incompetence, not because it's some organized campaign. \n\nLike Brenda down in the department office. She's supposed to sort the incoming lab packages, right? But last week, a shipment of specialized thermal paste I needed for the heat sinks just sat on her desk for four days. Four days! And when I finally went down there to find it myself, she didn't even apologize. She just started rambling about her daughter's wedding. Which she has been talking about non-stop since January, by the way. I don't even know why they're insisting on an outdoor reception in late October. Do you know how unstable the barometric pressure is around here that time of year? It's almost guaranteed to rain. And the tent rental company she hired—I looked them up—they have terrible reviews for not anchoring their tie-downs properly in saturated soil. If they get a wind gust over thirty miles an hour, that whole canopy is going to become a giant sail. I actually tried to explain the physics of it to her, just to be helpful, you know? Just drawing out a basic vector diagram on a sticky note. But she just looked at me like I was speaking Greek. People just do not appreciate basic meteorology.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt that ordinary events were sending you special messages meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Special messages? No, I don't think so. I mean, events are just events, right? They happen because of cause and effect, basic physics. There's no magical meaning behind a traffic light turning green or... or a bird landing on a windowsill. It's just probability and environmental factors. \n\nSpeaking of environmental factors, the HVAC system in my lab has been an absolute nightmare lately. The temperature fluctuates by almost three degrees throughout the day, which completely throws off my calibration curves. I've put in six work orders since Tuesday. Six! And the maintenance guy—Gary, I think his name is—finally shows up yesterday and tells me the system is \"working within normal parameters.\" Normal parameters! I had to pull up the sensor logs on my laptop and show him the exact timestamps of the thermal spikes. I even color-coded the graph so it would be impossible to miss. But he just kept wiping his forehead with this greasy rag and talking about how the compressor on the roof is twenty years old. \n\nWell, if it's twenty years old, replace it! It's not like the university doesn't have the endowment for it. Did you see the new bronze statue they just put up in front of the library? It must have cost at least eighty thousand dollars. And it's not even structurally sound! The center of gravity is completely off. I give it two harsh winters before the frost heave tilts the pedestal and the whole thing comes crashing down. I actually wrote an email to the dean of facilities about the tensile strength of the mounting brackets they used, but of course, no one ever replies to me. So no, I don't get any special messages from the universe. I just wish people would read the messages I actually send them.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone told you that your beliefs about what you can accomplish are clearly impossible?\n\nPatient: Impossible? I mean, maybe someone said that, but they just don't see the big picture. They told me I was taking on too much, but I genuinely believed I could rescue the entire multi-site project by myself. It was failing, obviously failing, because the other PIs were dragging their feet. I knew I could redesign the whole spectroscopy approach, mentor every single grad student better than their actual advisors were doing, and still beat the grant deadline. It was totally doable if people would just stop getting in my way. \n\nWhich, speaking of getting in the way, you wouldn't believe the traffic on I-95 this morning. It's like people forget how to use their turn signals the second they get on the highway. There was this one guy in a silver sedan who merged across three lanes without even looking. Just drifted right over. It's basic spatial awareness, really. If you're traveling at sixty-five miles per hour, your stopping distance is roughly three hundred feet. You can't just cut into someone's following distance and expect physics to magically suspend itself for you. \n\nAnd then, of course, the toll booth lanes were completely backed up because half the automated scanners were down. I don't understand why the state doesn't upgrade the RFID readers. The technology has been around for decades. I actually read a white paper last month about a new high-frequency transponder system they're using in Europe that has a 99.9% read accuracy even at high speeds. I mean, I could probably draft a proposal for the Department of Transportation in a weekend if I really sat down and did it. It's just a matter of optimizing the signal processing algorithms. But anyway, yeah, I could have saved that project if they had just let me handle it.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you had any beliefs that others said were clearly false or impossible?\n\nPatient: False or impossible? No, I don't think so. I mean, my ideas are grounded in reality, in data. I don't just make things up. If I say I can do something, it's because I've run the numbers and I know it's feasible. People might not understand the math, but that doesn't make it false. \n\nLike the math on my grocery bill the other day. I swear, inflation is just out of control. I went to get coffee beans—just the regular medium roast I always get, nothing fancy—and the price had jumped by almost three dollars. Three dollars! And the bag is smaller now, too. It used to be a full sixteen ounces, and now it's twelve. They think people won't notice, but I notice. It's called shrinkflation, and it's incredibly deceptive. I actually stood in the aisle and calculated the price per ounce difference on my phone. It's a twenty-five percent increase in cost for twenty-five percent less product. \n\nAnd don't even get me started on the produce section. The avocados were rock hard, and they were charging two-fifty each. Who pays two-fifty for an avocado that won't even be ripe for a week? By the time it softens up, the inside is usually all stringy and brown anyway. It's a complete gamble. I actually read an article about how they're trying to genetically modify avocados to stay ripe longer without oxidizing, which is fascinating from a biochemical standpoint. They're trying to suppress the polyphenol oxidase enzyme. If they could actually perfect that, it would revolutionize the supply chain. But no, I don't have any false beliefs. Just a very clear understanding of how things actually work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It was totally doable if people would just stop getting in my way. \n\nWhich, speaking of getting in the way, you wouldn't believe the traffic on I-95 this morning. It's like people forget how to use their turn signals the second they get on the highway. There was this one guy in a silver sedan who merged across three lanes without even looking. Just drifted right over. It's basic spatial awareness, really. If you're traveling at sixty-five miles per hour, your stopping distance is roughly three hundred feet. You can't just cut into someone's following distance and expect physics to magically suspend itself for you. \n\nAnd then, of course, the toll booth lanes were completely backed up because half the automated scanners were down. I don't understand why the state doesn't upgrade the RFID readers. The technology has been around for decades. I actually read a white paper last month about a new high-frequency transponder system they're using in Europe that has a 99.9% read accuracy even at high speeds. I mean, I could probably draft a proposal for the Department of Transportation in a weekend if I really sat down and did it. It's just a matter of optimizing the signal processing algorithms. But anyway, yeah, I could have saved that project if they had just let me handle it.\n\nDoctor: During this period, have you had any beliefs that others said were clearly false or impossible?\n\nPatient: False or impossible? No, I don't think so. I mean, my ideas are grounded in reality, in data. I don't just make things up. If I say I can do something, it's because I've run the numbers and I know it's feasible. People might not understand the math, but that doesn't make it false. \n\nLike the math on my grocery bill the other day. I swear, inflation is just out of control. I went to get coffee beans—just the regular medium roast I always get, nothing fancy—and the price had jumped by almost three dollars. Three dollars! And the bag is smaller now, too. It used to be a full sixteen ounces, and now it's twelve. They think people won't notice, but I notice. It's called shrinkflation, and it's incredibly deceptive. I actually stood in the aisle and calculated the price per ounce difference on my phone. It's a twenty-five percent increase in cost for twenty-five percent less product. \n\nAnd don't even get me started on the produce section. The avocados were rock hard, and they were charging two-fifty each. Who pays two-fifty for an avocado that won't even be ripe for a week? By the time it softens up, the inside is usually all stringy and brown anyway. It's a complete gamble. I actually read an article about how they're trying to genetically modify avocados to stay ripe longer without oxidizing, which is fascinating from a biochemical standpoint. They're trying to suppress the polyphenol oxidase enzyme. If they could actually perfect that, it would revolutionize the supply chain. But no, I don't have any false beliefs. Just a very clear understanding of how things actually work."
